That much hardware could ultimately mean extracting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , according to a recent preprint study by Falk and other researchers . <p> Falk and Ekchajzer also coauthored a study along with a group of other researchers assessing the environmental costs of training GPT-4 using Nvidia 's A100 GPU . They considered 16 different environmental impacts training can have -- ranging from high-profile issues like data centers ' energy use and carbon emissions to chemicals that escape into the environment from producing and discarding chips . The GPU chip itself dominated 10 categories , with manufacturing accounting for a majority of public health risks posed by toxic chemicals and nearly all of the cancer risk . <p> Since the very first computer chips were manufactured , they 've left behind a toxic legacy . It 's why Santa Clara County in the heart of Silicon Valley has more Superfund sites than any other in the US . These are places so polluted by the chemicals used in manufacturing -- some of which leaked out of underground storage tanks at chip factories to contaminate drinking water -- that the federal government has put them on the National Priorities List for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ chips at its new factory ( called a fab in industry speak ) in Arizona last year . The facility rises suddenly from the desert landscape around Phoenix when I drive by in October . It 's still expanding , flanked by red cranes that loom taller over the construction site and surrounding brush than anything other than power lines that connect it to the rest of the region . <p> Electricity demand is climbing in the US after more than a decade of flatlining -- not only because of energy-hungry data centers , but also because of a resurgence of domestic manufacturing . In places like the US where fossil fuels still dominate the electricity mix -- and where President Donald Trump is on a crusade to prop up coal and gas over wind and solar -- soaring electricity demand also means more air pollutants and planet-heating emissions . <p> Globally , the amount of electricity that AI chipmaking consumed jumped 350 percent from 2023 to 2024 , according to a report de Vries-Gao coauthored with Greenpeace last year . That resulted in a fourfold rise in carbon pollution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ annual emissions from a gas-fired power plant . Most of the GPUs are still manufactured in East Asia , where power grids are similarly heavily reliant on fossil fuels . <h> 453,000 metric tons of CO2 emissions <p> The energy used globally to make AI chips is estimated to have generated 453,600 metric tons of carbon dioxide emissions in 2024 , which is roughly equal to the weight of 4.5 Nimitz-class aircraft carriers . <p> The energy crunch was top of mind at Semicon West , the annual trade conference that was held in the new chip manufacturing hub of Phoenix last October . " Do we have enough energy to go around ? My answer is no , " Ajit Manocha , president and CEO of the industry group SEMI that organized the conference , said during his keynote talk at the event . A diagram of " unprecedented obstacles " the industry faces includes two resources crucial to making semiconductors : energy , and per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances ( PFAS ) , also known as " forever chemicals " linked to kidney and testicular cancer and other health @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ industrial facilities sprouting up to support chip manufacturing in Arizona was a big worry for some of the residents I met there last fall . The prospect of chemicals contaminating already drought-vulnerable water sources in the region only raises the stakes . <h> To the grave <p> DeDe Vries-Gao , who gained notoriety tracking the energy consumption and carbon pollution of crypto mining on the website Digiconomist , thought his work studying the environmental impact of data centers was coming to a close . <p> He started the website thinking , " There 's no nutrition label on digital applications , " he says . " There 's real-world impact for sure , but it 's not tangible and I think it 's really important that people are at least informed that , hold up , you know , when you 're using these applications , there is a cost . " But research by de Vries-Gao and others had already called a lot of attention to energy-hungry data farms used for crypto mining . The largest cryptocurrency after Bitcoin , Ethereum , had already switched to a vastly less @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to slash carbon emissions if crypto networks were willing . <p> Then as the hype around generative AI grew , his focus shifted . " One thing that crypto mining and AI have in common is the ' bigger is better ' dynamic , " he says . Data centers for AI posed many similar environmental challenges to crypto mines . But housing more powerful hardware , those risks can be even greater . Whereas specialized chips used for crypto mining perform the same repetitive task -- solving puzzles to validate transactions -- GPUs used for AI perform far more complex computing . <p> One unseen consequence is even more e-waste , including larger and more complex components used in AI data centers . De Vries-Gao 's latest study , published in February , estimates that AI servers could create between 0.131 million and 0.225 million tons of e-waste each year by 2030 . He says that 's comparable to all the e-waste produced by a country the size of Denmark , Norway , or Austria . It 's also a much more conservative estimate compared to a 2024 study @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ could reach between 1.2 million and 5 million tons by the end of the decade . <p> De Vries-Gao estimates a smaller amount of waste after factoring in supply chain constrictions , and because he sees the lifespan of a server growing beyond the three years that other researchers have used as a benchmark . But the amount of e-waste he projects will build up as a result of AI is still a lot , he tells me . " The total impact is significant , " he says . " Whether you 're talking about crypto miners or whether you 're talking about the big tech companies , they do n't take responsibility . " <h> 13.7 Empire State Buildings <p> A conservative estimate of e-waste produced by AI puts it at a little more than two-thirds of the weight of the Empire State Building each year by 2030 . In the most extreme estimate , total e-waste from AI could weigh as much as 13.7 Empire State Buildings by the end of the decade . <p> Only about 22 percent of the 68.3 million tons of e-waste accumulating @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ last comprehensive count published in 2024 . That rate is higher , 30 percent , in the Americas , where there are the most data centers . The rest of that e-waste enters the shadowy " informal " sector , often in lower-income countries where waste management facilities are less likely to meet high environmental , health , and safety standards . Sometimes called " backyard recycling , " people -- and potentially child laborers -- manually dismantle discarded electronics with little or no personal protective equipment . They might burn or melt devices down to extract raw materials that they can resell . Servers used for large language models hold valuable materials like gold , silver , and platinum , but also toxic materials including lead and the carcinogen chromium . Burning or burying those toxic substances can release harmful pollutants into the air or allow them to seep into groundwater . <p> Around 58 percent of e-waste coming from AI data centers is generated in North America , where there is a higher formal collection rate . And experts tell me that data center operators often work with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has not ratified the Basel Convention that limits the international trade of hazardous wastes , including e-waste . Investigations have found US recyclers sending e-waste abroad , where it might fall into the black hole of backyard recycling . <h> Gaming out the way forward <p> SomeSome of the researchers I talked to for this story say they also encounter questions about the need to focus on the environmental impact of AI when data centers globally only made up 1.5 percent of global electricity consumption in 2024 . <p> " Of course on a global scale , it 's not that much , " Falk says . When the U.S. president talks about " Debris Removal " and the " Brush " in forestry , he 's generally referring to prescribed or controlled burning , an Indigenous-pioneered environmental practice that slows the rapid spread of forest fires in dry climates by clearing out ground-level scraps ( twigs , dead plant matter , bushes , etc. ) that would otherwise provide extra kindling for the flames . You may remember the president invoking it repeatedly in California , after 2018 's deadly Camp Fire and during last year 's Palisades burns . He 's not wrong about the merits of controlled burns , but he 's wrong that they 're a straightforward panacea for wildfire season : Climate effects like drought , temperature hikes , and the spread of plant diseases strengthen fire conditions , and those climate consequences are bolstered by unceasing carbon and methane emissions that trap heat . Worse , burned trees release the carbon they were storing , then destroy other plant life that could keep that carbon absorbed . <p> Trump is also wrong about prescribed burns as the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ecosystems are far different from California 's ; our neighbor 's fires generally arise in massive boreal forests , which do not have debris or brush concerns because they 've never been logged or otherwise interfered with . Boreal forests are also supposed to burn on an infrequent basis , in order to open up certain pine cones and spread their seeds , or to clear out lower tree branches . But climate change has thrown the entire cycle out of whack , and these forests are now bursting into flame more frequently -- on the yearly basis that Trump and other Americans have come to experience this decade . And under a climate change -- driven heat dome that fuels more storms , the forests are all the more susceptible to inopportune lightning strikes . <p> Advertisement <p> However , because Trump and his fellow Republicans view climate change as a hoax , they have zero solutions for the widespread , fatal conflagrations of the type that Canada and the U.S. witness every season . Remember this when you see the commander in chief posting about " climate fraud @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) and international green policies . Or whenever you see Republicans doing everything they can to make life in a warming world worse than it already is , by stripping away heat protections from outdoor workers and shrinking public lands . They do n't care about Canada taking steps to mitigate global warming . What they do care about is using anything they can to attack one of our oldest allies -- even as this ill-advised battle weakens the United States economically and only encourages Canada to industrialize in a manner that will make future wildfire smoke far , far worse .

You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day . <p> Tariffs are back -- or at least the threat of them . Yesterday the Trump administration announced an additional 50 percent import duty on Canadian wine , hockey sticks , and other goods . But the tariffs wo n't actually take effect for almost a month , which means they might never : Donald Trump has a history of retreating in exchange for minor concessions . So before you get too worked up , here 's what you need to know . <p> Wait , I thought the Supreme Court struck down Trump 's tariffs . How is he allowed to do this ? <p> Good memory . The court threw out Trump 's import duties on many foreign products in February , saying he 'd overstepped his authority to enact tariffs ( which , with rare exceptions , are Congress ' turf ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ different presidential power , which permits " new or additional duties " on any country that " discriminates in fact against the commerce of the United States . " No previous president has used that provision to impose tariffs , though , so these may also face legal challenges . <p> OK , but why is Trump suddenly tariffing Canada again ? <p> The administration says it 's payback for Canadian duties on American cars , dairy products , and alcohol . " Canada has been very , very tough on us over the years , " Trump told reporters today . But the backstory is important : Earlier this month , the administration decided not to renew the U.S. -- Mexico -- Canada Agreement . ( Trump , a legendary flip-flopper , actually negotiated the trade deal in his first term but has since turned against it . ) Although the deal remains in force for the next decade , the move could reopen big parts of it to renegotiation . @@139162231 <h> The floodwaters spreading across Assam this week have killed almost a dozen people , displaced over 3.63 lakh and inundated 15 districts . <p> The floodwaters spreading across Assam this week have killed almost a dozen people , displaced over 3.63 lakh and inundated 15 districts from Sivasagar to Tinsukia . <p> A woman leads her livestock to a safer place after flooding in Morigaon district of Assam . ( PTI ) <p> The Army joined the National Disaster Response Force ( NDRF ) , State Disaster Response Force ( SDRF ) and fire and emergency services in relief operations in upper Assam as the Brahmaputra and its tributaries remained above danger levels . <p> The Assam State Disaster Management Authority ( ASDMA ) put the official death toll at 10 . Sivasagar was the worst-affected district , with nearly 1.58 lakh people displaced . <p> Rail services were disrupted , with floodwaters inundating tracks and forcing trains to be diverted , cancelled or short-terminated . Tea gardens too have been submerged , crops damaged and landslides reported near Guwahati . <p> But this is not an uncommon crisis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , according to the Rashtriya Barh Ayog , ( the National Flood Commission established by the Government in 1976 ) . <p> Annual floods have struck the state for decades , with particularly severe disasters in 2018 , 2020 , 2021 , 2022 and 2024 . The question of why requires looking not just at the rain , but at the river and Assam 's topography . <p> NDRF officials conduct a rescue operation in a flood-affected area in Assam . ( HT Print ) <h> A river that floods <p> Assam sits in the floodplain of the Brahmaputra , one of the world 's largest rivers that carries vast amounts of sediment . <p> The river begins on the Tibetan plateau , flows through Arunachal Pradesh and Assam , and is joined by more than 50 tributaries before eventually reaching the Bay of Bengal . Every monsoon , enormous volumes of water arrive from rainfall , Himalayan snowmelt and those tributaries cascading down from the hills . <p> According to the Assam government , Brahmaputra 's average annual discharge is about 20,000 cumec . For reference , one cumec @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ second . <p> Geography makes flooding easy here , but experts say rainfall is only one part of the story . The scale and frequency of flooding in Assam is also shaped by what has been done , and not done , over many decades . <h> A river slowed by its own sediment <p> Dhrubajyoti Sahariah , a geography professor and floodplain researcher at Gauhati University , told HT the fundamental problem is the speed and load of water being carried by the river . <p> After Pasighat , the river 's slope flattens all the way to Dhubri . As the water slows , it loses the energy to carry the sediment it has picked up in the mountains . <p> " It 's one of the highest sediment-carrying rivers in the world , " Sahariah said . <p> The result is that enormous quantities of sand and silt are deposited across the floodplain each year . Sahariah said the volume of sediment now coming down from the hills is increasing , and this was n't just a natural trend . <h> Deforestation steering the problem <p> A @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of landslides , is severe deforestation , Sahariah said . <p> Districts such as Lakhimpur , Biswanath and Dhemaji are particularly vulnerable , drained by rivers including the Siang , Jiadhal , Subansiri , Ranganadi and Dikrong , which flow from the hills composed of loose , sandy material that is inherently prone to erosion and landslides . <p> " A slight rain , high rain can trigger excessive landslide , excessive soil erosion . But it has increased due to deforestation , " he told HT . <p> Sahariah believes flood management has focused too heavily in downstream areas -- on embankments and structural interventions . " Contrary to working on the downstream , we should work on upstream , especially in the soil conservation measure , " he said . <p> He also pointed to reports of illegally cut logs accumulating in dam reservoirs as evidence of the scale of deforestation in the hills.estation upstream . <p> People wade through floodwaters and push two-wheelers along the submerged Jorhat-Sivasagar National Highway near Titabor , in Jorhat , Assam . ( PTI ) <h> Why Assam gets so much rain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ naturally wettest regions , where the monsoon arrives with considerable force . <p> Assam is among India 's wettest states , receiving an average annual rainfall of around 2,800-3,000 mm , with nearly 70% of it falling during the southwest monsoon ( June-September ) . While states such as Goa , Meghalaya , Sikkim and Arunachal Pradesh typically receive higher annual rainfall , Assam consistently ranks among the country 's top five to seven rainiest states , as per the India Meteorological Department ( IMD ) . <p> " Monsoon rainfall in Assam is due to the combined influence of moisture incursion from the Bay of Bengal due to south-westerly winds at the lower levels of the atmosphere , favourable topography , active monsoon synoptic systems like cyclonic circulation , and presence of monsoon trough over the northeast region , " Sunit Das , senior scientist at the IMD , told HT . <p> Sahariah also pointed out that in the longer term , climate crisis was affecting the intensity of rain , and consequently , the intensity of flooding too . <p> What climate change is doing , he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ peaks ... are higher ... greater devastation and extreme events are on the rise , " he said . <p> A man guides cattle to a safer place after floodwaters submerged low-lying areas , in Morigaon , Assam . ( PTI ) <h> 70 years of embankments <p> Assam has responded to annual flooding primarily by building embankments . According to the Rashtriya Barh Ayog , since the 1950s , the state has constructed 423 embankments along the Brahmaputra and its tributaries . Approximately 295 have already exceeded their intended lifespan -- making breaches increasingly common and the infrastructure increasingly unreliable . <p> Sahariah said this approach has a deeper limitation : the Brahmaputra is an avulsive river , one that keeps shifting its course . No embankment can hold a river that decides to flow somewhere else entirely . <p> " The Brahmaputra floodplain is a very young floodplain and it 's still not stable and very avulsive in nature . So , this type of direct structural measures may not always be feasible , " he said . <p> The toll accumulated over these decades of embankment-building reflects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 838 people died in Assam floods between 2013 and 2022 , with 2022 recording 181 deaths -- the highest in a decade . <p> Data from the Asian Disaster Reduction Centre ( ADRC ) , an international organisation that pushes for disaster resilience , said that the average number of people affected from flooding in Assam annually rose from around 8.6 lakh in the 1950s to over 45 lakh by the early 2000s . Economic losses in this time-frame increased over 120-fold . <h> What would help <p> Sahariah advocates a basin-wide approach -- managing the Brahmaputra as an integrated river system rather than responding to isolated points of flooding . But he argued that the shift must be behavioural as much as structural . <p> " We need to train the dwellers to how to live with the floods , " he said , pointing to the state 's Mising community , who have traditionally built homes designed for life on the floodplain . <p> " Somehow we are moving away from this traditional technology . We are adopting new house buildings or probably building homes in the floodplain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " he said . <h> When the water recedes <p> The destruction comes to fore when floodwaters withdraw . In Dhemaji , the Dikhari River changed course entirely in the latest floods , cutting through a settlement and destroying homes , farmland and public infrastructure . A railway bridge too collapsed in the region . <p> In the Lohit Khabolu area , villagers constructed a bamboo bridge themselves after damaged crossings left communities cut off from schools and hospitals . <p> " We have lost everything , " one villager told local outlet Northeast Now . " Our house , belongings and farmland have all been destroyed . We are now living in a temporary shelter built on raised bamboo platforms and surviving on the relief provided by the government . " <p> " The river never flowed through this part of the village earlier , " said another . " After changing its course , it swept away houses and cattle . " <p> For communities like theirs , the floods are not a seasonal interruption . They are a permanent rearrangement . <h> What the government has done @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Management Authority ( ASDMA ) , with the National Disaster Response Force ( NDRF ) , State Disaster Response Force ( SDRF ) , Army and local administration carrying out rescue and evacuation operations across the worst-hit districts . <p> Relief camps and distribution centres have been opened , while food , drinking water , medicines and other essential supplies are being distributed to affected families , as per PTI . State ministers have also been deputed to flood-hit districts to monitor relief operations , assess damage and coordinate rehabilitation efforts . <p> Union home minister Amit Shah has spoke to chief minister Himanta Biswa Sarma on June 29 about flood-related assistance . <h> Long-term measures <p> The Assam government says it is shifting from a relief-based approach to long-term flood mitigation . On July 15 , Himanta Biswa Sarma announced a ? 150-crore flood mitigation project for Jorabat , to be implemented as part of the proposed Guwahati Ring Road , The Assam Tribune reported . It was aimed at providing a permanent solution to recurring flash floods in the area . <p> Anita Goswami is a Senior Content @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and international news . @@139192637 <h> Battery-powered aircraft like the Electro will reduce emission levels only slightly , for they have limited range <p> TAXIING TOWARDS the runway , the propeller stops turning as your correspondent pulls the throttle back to await take-off . On a single-engined light aircraft , that is usually a worrying sign of engine failure . Yet this plane is powered by an electric motor and , just like when you come to a halt in a Tesla , the motor stops until it is needed again . On advancing the throttle the propeller springs back to life and the aircraft is soon briskly climbing , in an eerily quiet manner . <p> Electric aviation is taking off ( Unsplash ) <p> The two-seater Pipistrel Velis Electro is the first electric aircraft to be certified for pilot training . Besides its low noise , it has other unusual features . Instead of the lengthy series of engine checks a combustion-engine plane requires before take-off , the Electro is ready to go with the flick of four switches . Since it has few moving parts , it is also cheaper to maintain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ claim to be green , especially if recharged with renewable power . Topping up the battery takes just over an hour . <p> Returning to land back at Blackbushe Airport , south-west of London , the larger expanse of Farnborough Airport looms nearby . At the eponymous biennial air show , which opened on July 20th , sustainable flying was high on the agenda . Aviation accounts for around 2.5% of anthropogenic CO2 emissions , although overall its contribution to climate change is reckoned to be greater , since high-flying jets produce other heat-trapping emissions , such as nitrogen oxides . These are proving particularly tough to clean up . As a consequence , the aviation industry 's pledge to reach net-zero emissions by 2050 will probably be missed . <p> Battery-powered aircraft like the Electro will reduce emission levels only slightly , for they have limited range . This plane , built in Slovenia by Pipistrel , a light-aircraft producer owned by Textron , an American aerospace group , can typically fly for no more than 50 minutes , leaving time in hand for emergencies . <p> Nevertheless , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ schools , says NEBOair , a sustainable-aviation company which leases Electros to customers . The firm is developing a network of recharging stations to enable cross-country flights . Better batteries are also in the pipeline , driven by developments in the car industry . Those in the Electro can be swapped in the future for newer versions , so its range will increase over time . Yet it is unlikely batteries will ever be powerful enough , let alone light enough , to fly large numbers of passengers any great distance . <p> Moreover , batteries are a deadweight , meaning that , unlike a combustion-engine aircraft , an electric plane does not benefit from getting lighter as fuel is burnt . And commercial aircraft are required to have enough fuel to circle if a runway is unavailable or to divert to another airport , which might be a long way off . Batteries powerful enough to run even a small airliner for several hours , with sufficient reserve for emergencies , would be so heavy that few , if any , passengers could be carried . <p> Hence the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ electric motor and a combustion engine . Most passenger planes are powered by gas turbines , working either as jets or as turboprops , in which the turbines turn propellers . The first such hybrids are likely to be turboprops operating regional routes . There are several ways these could be made . <p> In Torrance , California , Heart Aerospace is developing the ES-30 , a 30-seat hybrid-electric aircraft for use on short-haul journeys . This would have an electric-only range of 200km but in hybrid form it could manage 800km . Having completed ground trials , the company is about to fly a demonstrator . This will use only a pair of electric motors , which will make it the largest battery-powered aircraft ever to take off . <p> In the production version , a gas turbine operating as an electrical generator would be incorporated into the housing containing the electric motors . This set up would act as a " series " hybrid , meaning the propellers would always be turned by the electric motors , with the turbine providing additional electricity when required . The combination @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expects the ES-30 to be some 40% cheaper to operate than existing regional jets . <p> Also in America , GE Aerospace is developing a different arrangement , known as a " parallel " hybrid , in collaboration with NASA , the country 's aerospace agency . This is a conventional turboprop with an attached electric motor that can be used to increase the turboprop 's power during certain phases of flight , such as a take-off and climbing . A modified Saab regional airliner , which has had one of its two engines replaced with the hybrid version for testing , flew across the North Atlantic , to appear at Farnborough . <p> Perhaps the most intriguing aspect of electric flight , though , are car-sized electric **29;1395;TOOLONG craft ( eVTOLs ) . These have long been talked of , but are at last starting to be deployed . Being able to hover , eVTOLs are likely to replace helicopters for some operations , and work as air taxis carrying handfuls of passengers on short flights . One , made by Vertical Aerospace , a British producer , became @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has started working on a hybrid version to provide greater range . A lot of development work remains ahead . But in one form or another electric aviation has started to reach for the sky . <p> Get the latest World News , breaking headlines and global updates from the US , UK , Pakistan , Bangladesh , Russia and other countries . When this occurs , warm surface water sloshes back towards South America , where the warming of surface water prevents the colder deepwater from upwelling . <p> El Ni ? o , which occurs irregularly and typically lasts for about a year , tends to increase global temperatures , which in turn can result in more extreme weather such as flooding . <p> This is because for every 1 ? rise in air temperature , the atmosphere can hold around seven per cent more moisture , leading to more intense and heavy rainfall . @@139193635 <h> Mastodonsaurus lived about 240 million years ago during the Middle Triassic period . It was an underwater ambush hunter , preying mainly on fish and smaller amphibians <p> Volunteers clean the skeleton of a Mastodonsaurus , a huge amphibian that walked the Earth 240 million years ago , at the Copernicus Science Center in Warsaw , Poland . Photo Credit : AP <p> Volunteers armed with chisels , brushes and protective goggles chip away at rock encasing the bones of a massive prehistoric amphibian in a glass-walled laboratory on the ground floor of Warsaw 's Copernicus Science Centre . <p> On the other side of the wall , visitors watch the work unfold on the bones of the nearly six metre ( 20 foot ) -long skeleton . Children point with excitement while parents contemplate the creature the massive bones once belonged to . The painstaking restoration is part of an unusual public science project . More than 2,000 volunteers are helping Polish scientists restore the skeleton of the Mastodonsaurus , a fearsome aquatic predator from around the dawn of the dinosaur age . <p> The work is meticulous @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ millimetre with a pneumatic chisel , whose needle gently breaks away rock while ultraviolet light reveals where the bone ends and the surrounding rock begins . The volunteers are managers , cleaners , tattoo artists -- not geologists or palaeontologists . They receive training and special protective gear , before they begin two-hour-long chiselling sessions under supervision . <p> " I imagine that those bones were lying underground for 240 million years and I am the first human on earth to touch them , " said Tatyana Kozyk , the coordinator of the volunteer teams . " I 'm still trying to wrap my head around this experience . " <h> An ancient aquatic predator <p> One of the largest amphibians ever to exist , the Mastodonsaurus lived about 240 million years ago during the Middle Triassic , while the earliest dinosaurs were appearing but before they came to dominate Earth . It was an underwater ambush hunter , preying mainly on fish and smaller amphibians . <p> Scientists believe it could grow to nearly six metres ( 20 feet ) in length . It had a broad , flat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ appearance resembling a crocodile 's , though it belonged to a different branch of the vertebrate family tree . When the restoration is complete , it will be the largest exhibited in a museum anywhere in the world , said Tomasz Sulej , a palaeontologist and professor at the Institute of Palaeobiology at the Polish Academy of Sciences . Its skeleton weighs 1,300 Kg ( 2,865 pounds ) . <p> Mr. Sulej and his colleague Wojciech Pawlak from the Faculty of Geology at the Warsaw University are credited with discovering the skeleton in southern Poland in 2023 and coming up with the idea to invite volunteers to help with its restoration . Mr. Sulej says there are no other known Mastodonsaurus skeletons this large anywhere else in the world , and the discovery promises to help scientists learn more about the species . <p> " The structure of the body is different from what we knew so far , " he said . " This is information that is completely new to the scientific world . " Other Mastodonsaurus skeletons have been found elsewhere , including in Germany and Russia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to 3 metres ( 6 ? to 10 feet ) long . <h> A chance to reflect on the past and future <p> Dorota Serafin , a 51-year-old manager from Warsaw who volunteers for the project , said working with the skeleton of an ancient animal is an experience that is at the same time " relaxing and philosophical , even mystical " . Ms. Serafin said she plans to return for other sessions and eventually bring her family to Copernicus when the restored skeleton is exhibited , which is expected to happen in October or November . <p> In addition to the startling experience of touching the remains of an animal that lived hundreds of millions of years ago , contact with the Mastodonsaurus offers an opportunity to reflect on the future , those involved in the project said . <p> The amphibian inhabited Earth 's waters after the largest known mass extinction and just before the dinosaurs appeared . As humanity contemplates the potentially catastrophic effects of climate change , Mr. Sulej says the story of the Mastodonsaurus offers some hope . " The Mastodonsaurus lived after the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ always manages , " Mr. Sulej said . " It 's not true that humans can destroy life on this planet . @@139257320 <p> AI data centers are becoming increasingly unpopular as they drive up utility prices , harm the environment , and impact quality of life for those living nearby . A recent Gallup poll found that 70% of Americans oppose local construction , with nearly half saying they strongly oppose new data centers coming to their neighborhoods . Recent heatwaves have made these issues all the more apparent , with data centers getting access to emergency energy backups at times when demand is high . <p> Frustration from locals is n't the only obstacle data centers are facing amid record-breaking heatwaves across the world . These facilities already run hot and require massive amounts of water to cool their equipment , with Amazon 's data centers reportedly using 2.5 billion gallons in a single year . Heatwaves mean the equipment needs more cooling , driving up operational costs . That 's only going to get worse as climate change brings about more heatwaves , extreme weather , droughts , and more . <p> According to the World Economic Forum ( WEF ) , the cost of running data centers could increase @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The WEF makes suggestions for preparing them for climate-related disruption and disasters , but even if scientists can make cooling systems more efficient , it does n't change the fact that data centers are currently being impacted by a climate crisis they are also exacerbating -- and a lot of people have had enough . <h> Heatwaves are making data centers even less popular <p> Natalie Behring/Getty Images <p> Data center hubs like Slough , England , are literally feeling the heat , with one person who works in the town telling The Guardian it was like having something " pinching your body and burning your skin . " Not everyone in Slough , which is about 20 miles outside of London and is already home to dozens of active data centers , believes the facilities are worsening the heatwaves , but early research suggests they are to blame . One study ( which has not yet been peer reviewed ) estimates that data centers increase the local temperature by 3.6 degrees Fahrenheit , creating what the researchers call the data heat island effect . <p> Any further temperature @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not the only reason people around the world are turning against data centers . In Utah , hundreds of protesters came out in opposition to a massive AI data center announced for Box Elder County . Many fear the project will consume water in a state where Governor Spencer Cox declared a state of emergency due to " extreme drought conditions " in May 2026 . <p> The backlash against new data centers has had some success . According to Data Center Watch , protests blocked $130 billion worth of projects in just Q1 2026 as grassroots opposition against data centers has gained steam . It 's starting to translate into political change . New York Governor Kathy Hochul signed a first-in-the-nation statewide moratorium on new hyperscale data centers , referring to those that use 50 megawatts of power or more , giving the state a year to establish regulations to protect residents and the environment . It 's clear that anger towards AI data centers is growing across the country and the political spectrum , and heatwaves are making their environmental and financial costs all the more apparent . 
@@139258722 <h> Gerald Winegrad : A global warming apocalypse approaches COMMENTARY <h> Share this : <p> Smoke from Canadian wildfires shrouds the Baltimore skyline which combined with 100-degree heat , caused a Code Purple dangerous air quality alert . Global warming drought and extreme heat triggered the widespread and intense fires that on July 17 , included nearly 900 active blazes nationwide . About 6.9 million acres had burned across the country in 2026 . ( Kevin Richardson/The Sun ) <p> Despite decades of dire scientific warnings to curb global warming gases , we are now facing apocalyptic meteorological changes threatening life on Earth . The proof is all around us . A " meteorological Armageddon " was a hypothetical 2050 scenario popularized by a mock TV forecast . It was chillingly exceeded during record-smashing global warming including blistering heat waves in late June and July . The sudden acceleration of hot planetary climate is driving increasing deaths and disasters in excess of traditional , cautious modeling . <p> Early in my 57-year environmental advocacy career , I learned the critical importance of establishing credibility with sound science , not resorting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tactics . It was essential that science guide my activism . <p> In the case of global warming , it is hard to overstate the urgency . In a 2022 U.N . report , reviewed and agreed upon by 195 countries , more than 200 scientists demanded immediate action or the Earth would become uninhabitable . They predicted low-lying cities would be permanently flooded , and " unprecedented heat waves , terrifying storms , widespread water shortages and the extinction of a million species of plants and animals . " They warned that record-high harmful carbon emissions put the world on a fast track to disaster . <p> The 2015 Paris Climate Agreement set two primary goals to prevent disasters : ( 1 ) the world 's nations must strive to limit increases in global temperatures to 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels , an ambitious target that would significantly reduce risks ; and ( 2 ) keep increases well below 2 C , which was considered the maximum increase to avoid the most severe climate impacts . <p> Unfortunately , global temperatures exceeded 1.5 C several times over the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Agreement 's goal , which was for a 20- to 30-year span . Current emission trajectories suggest a breach may occur in February 2029 . Worse , many studies predict reaching the 2 C increase by 2037 if emissions remain close to current levels . <p> In 2025 , global temperature averaged 1.47 C above pre-industrial levels , continuing an extraordinary streak of the last 11 years becoming the 11 warmest on record , and ocean heating continued unabated . <p> The alarming effects of global warming are upon us , as documented by the Lancet medical journal 's 2025 report , Countdown on Health and Climate Change . Produced in collaboration with the World Health Organization , the report documents more than a million lives lost annually from global warming . Extreme heat was the major cause , with infants and older adults facing 20 heat wave days per year -- a nearly fourfold increase since 2001 , with 84% of heat wave days attributed to global warming . <p> The rate of heat-related deaths surged 23% to 546,000 a year . Biological aging research indicates that repeated exposure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health conditions . Other species are also threatened . <p> Droughts and heat waves pushed an additional 124 million people into moderate to severe food insecurity , leading to many more deaths . Wildfires , fueled by warming weather and drought , are increasing and growing in size . A record-high 154,000 estimated deaths occurred from wildfire smoke-derived small particulate matter air pollution . <p> Global warming is a major factor in increasing transmission of vector-borne diseases , including from mosquitoes and ticks . Rising temperatures directly accelerate their life cycles , increase biting rates and shorten incubation periods of viruses within them . The Lancet report found that potential global dengue transmission rose by up to 49% since the 1950s . A record 12,000 dengue-related deaths were reported in 2024 . <p> An aedes mosquito , primary carrier of dengue , Zika , chikungunya , and yellow fever . Rising temperatures are expanding their population and geographic range in the U.S. as well as their diseases . ( CDC ) <p> Warmer temperatures have led to increasing transmissions of Lyme disease , malaria , Zika virus and West Nile @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and increased flooding , spiking increases in cholera , gastroenteritis , typhoid and cryptosporidiosis , as well as flesh-eating diseases in the Chesapeake Bay and elsewhere . <p> Extreme heat , prolonged pollen seasons and smoke from climate-fueled wildfires worsen asthma and COPD . The Lancet report found that air pollution from the burning of fossil fuels was attributable to 2.5 million deaths globally each year . <p> The prestigious Lancet used 128 experts from 71 collaborating institutions in over 50 countries to produce its report . It found that excessive heat exposure cost $640 billion in lost labor hours , equivalent to $1.09 trillion in productivity . Older adults ' heat-related deaths cost $261 billion . The report concluded that failure to curb GHG emissions is increasing human deaths and affecting livelihoods <p> Western European countries reported more than 10,000 excess deaths during the record-breaking heat wave that engulfed the continent in late June . The vast majority -- more than 9,000 -- were aged 65 and above . Extreme heat can kill by causing heat stroke or aggravating cardiovascular and respiratory diseases . Scientists concluded that the late-June @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ human-caused climate change , which is making heat waves more frequent and intense . <p> In the U.S. , exposure to climate-driven heat is extensive . Nearly two-thirds of Americans live in counties experiencing a dramatic rise in extreme heat days compared with historical baselines . In 2024 , the average person was exposed to 16 days of dangerous heat that would not have occurred without climate change , with infants and older adults facing over 20 heat wave days per person -- a fourfold increase over 20 years . <p> A paddle on a flooded downtown Annapolis . The City is spending $87 million on the City Dock Resiliency Project , the largest infrastructure project in the city 's history , to combat flooding which has greatly increased in frequency and depth from sea level rise linked to global warming . @@139281826 <h> I dreamt of building rockets until my aunt 's illness inspired me to build an AI startup that 's raised nearly $10 million <p> Copy **45;675;TOOLONG ... bolt iconAn icon in the shape of a lightning bolt.Impact Link <p> SaveSaved <p> This story is available exclusively to Business Insider subscribers . Become an Insider and start reading now.Have an account ? . <p> This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Lily Vittayarukskul , 30 , cofounder and CEO of Waterlily . It has been edited for length and clarity . <p> When I was a kid , I was fascinated by the universe and wanted to understand how the world worked . Science taught me that even the biggest , most intimidating questions could be broken down , understood , and eventually solved . <p> I also grew up with limited resources , and working in my parents ' factory after school taught me how hard families work to create stability , and how much discipline and resourcefulness it takes to build something from very little . <p> At 11 , I entered a NASA competition with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ got the chance to visit Kennedy Space Center . Later , I interned at NASA as a young teenager . For years , I thought aerospace engineering was my future . <p> Then my aunt was diagnosed with cancer , and the trajectory of my entire family changed almost overnight . <p> My aunt did n't speak much English , so my mom translated at doctor 's appointments . My family quickly learned that there were expenses we had n't expected . We also learned that caring for someone with a serious illness extended far beyond medical appointments . <p> I was 16 when she became sick . What I remember most was n't a single diagnosis or conversation . It was the quiet . <p> Everyone in my family was exhausted , moving from work to doctor 's appointments to caregiving without ever stopping to talk about what was happening . We were all operating on autopilot because there was simply too much to do . <h> Watching my family unravel changed my definition of impact <p> Courtesy of Waterlily <p> My mom became my aunt 's primary caregiver @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ whenever we could . I went to my parents ' factory after school , often staying until 10 p.m. or 11 p.m. before going home . <p> Only later did I realize the full cost . <p> Our family relationships had become strained . Financially , we had almost nothing left . There was blame , tension , and isolation that lingered long after my aunt died . <p> Growing up in a first-generation immigrant family , I 'd always understood that money mattered . My parents worked incredibly hard , yet I learned early that hard work alone does n't guarantee financial security . You have to create economic value . That lesson shaped almost every decision I 've made since . <p> After my aunt died , I started asking a different question : Instead of wondering how I could build better rockets , I wondered why families like mine had to navigate one of life 's hardest moments with so little support . <h> I realized no one was solving the problem I cared about most <p> Vittayarukskul cofounded her company to help families avoid what hers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ engineering and building things , but I realized I could apply those skills somewhere that felt much more personal . <p> No one seemed to be solving the problem the way I thought it needed to be solved . That 's why I cofounded Waterlily . <p> Waterlily is an AI-powered long-term care planning platform that helps people estimate when they may need long-term care , how much it could cost , and how to pay for it based on their health , finances , and family situation . The platform provides personalized projections and funding strategies -- including insurance , savings , and caregiving plans -- to help families prepare for the financial and logistical challenges of aging . Since launching , the company has raised $9.2 million . <p> The work has been much harder than I imagined . <p> Healthcare does n't exist in one industry . Neither does aging . We work across healthcare , insurance , and financial services simultaneously , each with different incentives and different ways of operating . Every week seems to introduce a new layer of complexity . <p> There were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it out . What kept me going , though , was the same question I asked myself after my aunt died . If we 're not solving this problem , who will ? <p> After speaking with thousands of families , one thing surprised me . I 'd assumed my family 's experience was unusually devastating . Instead , I found that many families who experience serious long-term care needs face financial strain and relationship breakdowns . The details vary , but the emotional toll often looks remarkably similar . <h> Building this company changed how I think about my own future <p> Starting Waterlily has changed me in ways I did n't expect . <p> I 've become much more financially literate than I ever thought I 'd need to be . Building Waterlily has required me to develop deep expertise across insurance , retirement planning , healthcare , and financial services . Understanding aging means understanding how all of those systems -- and the decisions families make within them -- fit together . <p> When I was younger , I thought success meant solving interesting technical problems . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people regain a sense of control during moments when life feels completely uncertain . <p> If my aunt were alive today , I do n't think she would care very much about the technical details of the platform . But I also think she 'd ask me two questions : <p> Are you helping people ? <p> Is this business sustainable ? <p> I 'd like to think she 'd be proud of both answers . <p> Waterlily offers a free tool on its site that provides personalized insights , based on more than half a billion data points , to better understand what aging and long-term care could look like . <h> Business Insider tells the innovative stories you want to know <h> Business Insider tells the innovative stories you want to know <h> Business Insider tells the innovative stories you want to know <h> Business Insider tells the innovative stories you want to know <p> You 're currently following this author ! @@139311137 <h> New monkey species , orange-lipped Likweli monkey , known as ' branch shaker , ' identified in DRC <h> An FAU statement described the monkey as " draped in glossy black fur with cape-like shoulders , a long sweeping tail and a vivid orange-cream patch around the mouth and nose . ( photo credit : Daniel Rosengren , Frankfurt Zoological Society/Handout ) ByDANYA SAPERSTEINJULY 26 , 2026 07:41 <p> International scientists , including researchers from Florida Atlantic University ( FAU ) and Yale , have identified a new species of monkey with a silky black coat and orange cream skin around its mouth in the rainforest of the Democratic Republic of the Congo . <p> While the monkey has been seen before , both by locals who call it " Likweli " in one local dialect and " branch shaker " in another and by conservation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was not declared a new species until a paper published July 15 in the journal PLOS One determined they were distinct from other monkeys and named the species Colobus congoenisis . <p> Co-author of the paper Junior Amboko chose the name to recognize " the Congo Basin 's remarkable natural heritage and , we believe , marking the first primate named after the Democratic Republic of Congo itself - underscoring both its global importance and local pride , " Amboko said in a statement . <p> The photo in 2008 was far from enough to study the species , not even containing the whole animal , and the Likweli monkey was not seen again until 2018 , when field researcher and co-author of the paper , Jean Pierre Kapale , managed to photograph one , according to Smithsonian . <p> Kapale and his team spent the next 10 months conducting dedicated surveillance patrols and had seven more sightings . More researchers joined in the attempt to discover more about the Likweli , and between 2018 and 2022 , researchers recorded 114 field observations of the monkeys . <p> Two ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , discovered in Lomami National Park , sit on a tree branch , in the Democratic Republic of the Congo , in this undated handout image . ( credit : Daniel Rosengren , Frankfurt Zoological Society/Handout ) <h> Researchers describe monkeys as watchful <p> The monkey has a " quiet and watchful nature , " Kate Detwiler and Junior Amboko , two other coauthors of the paper , told BBC 's Discover Wildlife . <p> " When we encounter a group , the monkeys do n't usually flee as many other primates do . Instead , they climb higher into the canopy and simply watch us . It often feels as though we 're studying each other , " they said . <p> Researchers also examined skin , skeleton , and tissue samples from three dead monkeys that had been confiscated from hunters . <p> An FAU statement described the monkey as " draped in glossy black fur with cape-like shoulders , a long sweeping tail and a vivid orange-cream patch around the mouth and nose , " with a " striking , mask-like appearance unlike any known colobus monkey @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ species , weighing only about 15 pounds , about as much as a small dog . <h> Monkeys provide new insight into monkey evolution <p> While genetic studies proved that the Likweli monkey was part of the genus Colobus , they also provided new insights into African primate evolution , according to Kate Detwiler , Ph.D. , co-author and associate professor at FAU . <p> " Its closest known relative is Colobus satanas , found more than 1,200 kilometers away in west-central Africa . Yet our genetic evidence shows the two species diverged roughly 4 to 5 million years ago , marking one of the oldest known evolutionary splits within the Colobus lineage , " Detwiler said . <p> The Environment and Climate Change portal is produced in cooperation with the Goldman Sonnenfeldt School of Sustainability and Climate Change at Ben-Gurion University of the Negev . What Hochul and Mamdani have in common is Abundance . <p> At Abundance New York , which I co-founded in 2024 , we support politicians and policies targeting an end to scarcity : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the paucity of renewable energy driving up costs and emissions , the hamstrung public sector that struggles to distribute housing vouchers and food stamps -- let alone universal childcare or healthcare . <p> These crises cross ideological lines , and ( to paraphrase the mayor ) for far too long , New York leaders left , right , and center have rejected the solutions New York needs . <p> Centrists have said no to bigger government , from public renewable energy to universal social programs . Leftists have said no to private sector and supply-side interventions , from market-rate housing to new nuclear power . ( Centrists often say no to those , too , but because of concerns about " neighborhood character " rather than corporate power ) . <p> Abundance is committed to the progress and pluralism that are the best of cities , imagining a future of radical plenty . Abundance wants to build skyscrapers , subway lines , and solar farms , and it demands tearing down the walls of nativism and NIMBYism . That requires saying " yes , and " to both strong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rather than the size of a check written or the brashness of a press release . <p> Some recent punditry has declared that Abundance lost the battle for the Democratic Party 's future . But that view wrongly supposes that Abundance was meant to be its own electoral lane . Instead , across lanes , more leaders are driving in an Abundance direction . <p> Our Abundance Agenda to lower costs and improve quality of life is neither a centrist response to the left nor a watered down democratic socialism . It 's not a point on the left-right spectrum at all : it 's a different axis . <p> That 's why Mamdani 's " sewer socialism " and " pothole politics " can resonate with Hochul 's " Let them Build " agenda . Both leaders are focused on breaking chokeholds , improving performance , and delivering for New Yorkers . <p> This might sound like common sense ; who does n't want to cut red tape ? But New York built the Empire State Building in a fraction of the time it now takes to hold public @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ study trash containerization . We 've lost our ability to dream big and make those dreams come true . <p> We must grow the bloc of New Yorkers who want to change that -- whether they 're members of the DSA or the Partnership for New York City . <p> But because Abundance cuts across the conventional left-right axis , New York politicians left , right , and center can all be on the wrong side of it . <p> Will the new socialists in the Legislature follow the mayor in embracing market rate housing and prioritizing public sector excellence ? Will Wall Street and state Democratic Party Chair Jay Jacobs follow the governor 's lead in supporting the mayor when Abundance is on the agenda ? <p> Or will we focus more on factional fights than on the fight for a better future ? <p> Kessler is the co-founder of Abundance New York . @@139314328 <p> The downstream side of the Glen Canyon Dam . Lake Powell , the dam 's reservoir , is only 27 percent full . <p> Jim West/UCG/Universal Images Group via Getty Images <p> Lake Powell , the second-largest reservoir in the U.S. , is nearing critically low water elevation levels , the latest data shows . <p> As of Monday , water levels at Lake Powell measured at 3,524.3 feet above sea level , according to the U.S. Bureau of Reclamation tracker . <p> Minimum , or " dead , " power pool -- in which the water is too low to spin the hydroelectric turbines and can no longer produce hydropower -- starts at 3,490 feet of elevation , Peter Soeth , public affairs lead at the Bureau of Reclamation , told ABC News . Elevation at Lake Powell is currently about 34 feet above the minimum power pool . <p> The downstream side of the Glen Canyon Dam . Lake Powell , the dam 's reservoir , is only 27 percent full . <p> Jim West/UCG/Universal Images Group via Getty Images <p> Since the early 1960s , when @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Canyon Dam , the reservoir has never been lower than 5.26 million acre feet in live storage -- or the water that can flow by gravity from the reservoir through the various tubes and tunnels that can be used for reservoir release , Jack Schmidt , director of the Center for Colorado River Studies at Utah State University , told ABC News . <p> Lake Powell currently has about 5.52 million acre-feet of large storage and has lost about 4,800 acre-feet of water per day since June 1 , Schmidt said . <p> Reservoir operations can get " very complicated " once elevation drops below 3,500 feet and live storage drops below 4.3 million acre-feet , Schmidt said . At that point , the Bureau of Reclamation would " be seriously concerned , " he added . <p> The reservoir could reach a " dead pool " when its elevation drops to 3,370 feet , at which point water can no longer flow past Glen Canyon Dam by gravity . <p> In a dead pool , about 240 feet of water would be trapped at the bottom of the canyon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on it in Arizona , California and Nevada , the Lake Powell Chronicle reported . <p> More than two-thirds of the West remains in drought , with much of the Colorado River Basin experiencing severe to extreme conditions , according to the U.S. Drought Monitor . <p> There could be some short-term relief in the near future . Above-average precipitation is favored across much of the West , according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration 's Climate Prediction Center . Monsoon season and an intensifying El Ni ? o are also underway and could bring more chances for rain to the region . <p> However , any additional precipitation will not offset the long-term factors driving water shortages like prolonged drought conditions and historically low seasonal snowpack , according to experts . <p> Water levels in Lake Powell are several hundred feet below full capacity at the Glen Canyon Dam on June 7 , 2026 in Page , Arizona . <p> George Frey/Getty Images <p> Water supplies in Lake Powell are currently dwindling dangerously close -- about 4 feet -- to the previous record set in April 2023 at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rate of decline has leveled off in recent weeks , running around 2 feet higher than previous projections , which suggests that mitigation efforts could be helping somewhat . <p> In May , the Colorado River Basin Forecast Center projected that Lake Powell could drop to a new record-low level by July , based on expected inflow rates between April and July . <p> Experts predict that a new record-low could be reached at Lake Powell in August . <p> " If you do the simple math , it would suggest that Lake Powell will set a record for its lowest point in about a month and a half , " Schmidt said . <p> Lake Mead , the largest reservoir in the country , is currently measuring at 1,042.8 feet above sea level -- just over 2 feet above its record-low level . It continues to steadily decline , with the water level dropping around 5 feet in June , Bureau of Reclamation data shows . Lake Mead could approach the record of 1,041 feet above sea level set in July 2022 by the end of the month . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ River system underscores the need for new operating guidelines , Soeth said . <p> The basin is severely over-allocated , experts say . The water rights were established during a period of the 20th century when water levels were " unusually high , " Erica Fleishman , director of the Oregon Climate Change Research Institute , told ABC News in May . <p> Since the mid-21st century , runoff in the Colorado River Basin has been about 13 to 15% less than in the mid to late 20th century , Schmidt said . Year over year , the basin has experienced reduced supply , often combined with a string of very dry years , and the river system has never been able to adequately rebuild its supply . <p> " During those very dry years , we deplete reservoir storage because society is not able to reduce its use at the same rate that nature reduces its supply , " Schmidt said . <p> The lower basin states have submitted an allocation plan for consideration , but there is not yet a consensus plan among all of the states , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the Global Institute of Sustainability and Innovation at Arizona State University , told ABC News . <p> If the states ca n't reach a negotiated agreement , the Bureau of Reclamation will need to intervene to make a final decision , Schmidt said . <p> " The Department and Reclamation remain committed to reducing the collective risk of both Lake Powell and Lake Mead falling below critical elevations to protect critical infrastructure so that it can continue to function as authorized to meet the water and hydropower needs of the basin , " Soeth said . <p> The Bureau of Reclamation is set to release another update to the projections this week . 
@@139330138 <p> The left-wing indoctrination myth has always been just that -- a myth . Ed Burmila , Jeffrey Sachs and David A. Bell , among others , cite ample evidence that the political views of professors are n't particularly radical in the first place and that those views have a negligible effect on students ' own ( also see here and here ) . <p> In our forthcoming book , Getting Learning Right : The Promise of Higher Education ( MIT Press ) , we share the results of a study that followed 20 Northeastern University students every semester from their first year through graduation . The study confirms what others have found : With few exceptions , professors welcomed diverse viewpoints and insisted that students formulate their own analyses and views . The students also described being influenced by a variety of people , not just professors -- classmates , friends , family members , advisers , co-workers , bosses and more . Some intentionally chose mentors with diverse perspectives . <h> Most Popular <p> Recently , we caught up with eight of our study participants , now five @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that certain professors , peers , courses and especially experiential learning opportunities had a lasting influence on them . What did surprise us : When they spoke about their experiences , they described the value of college largely in terms of helping them to identify and live their values . From their perspective , we in higher education are in the values-formation business -- just not in the way our critics allege . <p> To be clear : These alumni were not saying they had been taught specific values , but rather that college gave them time , opportunities and support to figure out what kinds of lives they wanted to lead and what contributions they wanted to make to their families , communities and the world . We want to introduce you to three of these remarkable young people . They help us understand that in fact , value formation is a crucial purpose -- and thus value proposition -- for postsecondary education . @@139330439 <p> The thousands of visitors and campers in Colorado 's Big Thompson Canyon had only minutes to find higher ground . <p> Around dinner time on July 31 , 1976 , a storm system stalled high in the sky above the center of the canyon , which stretches between Estes Park and Loveland . Over the course of an hour , the clouds unleashed more than 7 inches of rain , swelling the river encased in the narrow canyon by a foot every three minutes . <p> By the end of the storm the following day , the system had dumped the equivalent of the area 's normal yearly rainfall on the canyon . <p> The torrent killed 144 people , injured more than 200 and stranded hundreds in the canyon , some of whom had to be rescued by helicopter . The flood destroyed more than 400 homes and 50 businesses as well as bridges , roads , power lines -- nearly everything in its path . The damage totaled $234 million in today 's dollars . Weary residents make their way past demolished cars and a water-battered motel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Aug. 1 , 1976 . ( File photo by Ernie Leyba/The Denver Post ) <p> Fifty years later , with the anniversary coming this Friday , the Big Thompson flood remains one of Colorado 's deadliest natural disasters . The catastrophe spurred changes to emergency response protocols and prompted deeper research into mountain floods . Larimer County officials installed warning signs in the canyon and more securely attached the canyon road to bedrock to keep it from washing away in future floods . They also banned the construction of new buildings in the floodway . <p> " It touched the social fabric of our community and of the nation , " Larimer County Sheriff John Feyen said . <p> Since the flood , technological improvements have made alerting people to flash floods easier and more effective . Weather forecasting , too , has become more accurate and specific . <p> While those changes make predicting and responding to a flash flood more seamless , they do n't change the whims of Mother Nature . <p> " Could the Big Thompson flood happen again ? Of course it could , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ U.S. Geological Survey who studied the 1976 flood . <p> And there 's plenty of uncertainty , he added : " It could be bigger -- or it could be smaller . " <p> Dogs , goods and people traverse the Big Thompson River in 1976 . ( File photo by Ernie Leyba/The Denver Post ) <h> Moving boulders like sand <p> In 1976 , people in the canyon who survived the flood described hearing what sounded like a train coming at them . It was a surge of water carrying boulders , exploding propane tanks and houses . <p> Emergency responders used helicopters to drop sleeping bags , food and clothes to stranded survivors , as well as leaflets instructing them to gather on high ground and wait for extraction . <p> In the days after the flood , a National Guard helicopter deposited Jarrett in the canyon , which had become inaccessible by car . From a camp jokingly called the " Drake Hilton , " he and other researchers spent weeks in the canyon conducting a post-mortem on the floodwaters . <p> Jarrett and his coworkers later @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Big Thompson River at the canyon mouth charged down at 31,200 cubic feet per second -- nearly four times the previous maximum flow recorded . The torrent 's force moved boulders more than 20 feet in diameter and weighing more than 275 tons , equivalent to the heft of more than 150 Subaru Outbacks . <p> Smaller boulders , around 3 or 4 feet in diameter , were transported downstream like sand , he said . <p> To explain the river 's power , Jarrett tells people to imagine 31,200 basketballs , each measuring a little less than a cubic foot , coming toward them every second . <p> Evacuees of the Big Thompson River flood in 1976 . ( File photo by John Sunderland/The Denver Post ) <p> In some places , the river 's water rose from a foot deep to more than 20 feet in less than an hour . Standing waves reached 10 feet above the water surface . <p> The Big Thompson flood caused a surge in research on flash flooding in the Rocky Mountains , Jarrett said . Findings have included that flash floods @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ below that altitude -- like where the Big Thompson lies . <p> The disaster also prompted the study of ancient floods as a way to gauge how common large flash floods are in specific areas -- a field called paleoflood hydrology . Jarrett later found that , in some areas of the Big Thompson River basin , the 1976 flood was the largest since the retreat of glaciers 10,000 years ago . <p> " We in the hydro-meteorological community , we 've learned a lot , " he said . " But to convey that to the public is the most difficult thing to do . " <h> ' We 're better positioned to recover ' <p> Dozens of static signs now instruct visitors to the Big Thompson Canyon to climb to safety in case of a flood . Electronic signs stand by to warn travelers of impending weather . <p> Technological improvements have made it easier to alert people to an impending disaster . Larimer County and the National Weather Service can send an alert to cell phones across a geographic area . <p> " You 're holding a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up your phone , " said Feyen , the sheriff . <p> But law enforcement will still go in person to warn people , he said , especially in areas like the canyon where cell phone service is iffy . <p> The 1976 flood was one in a series of disasters that prompted the creation of incident command centers -- which serve as a central hub for decision-making for all agencies responding to a crisis , Hodges said . The emergency system in the 1970s was primarily focused on nuclear threats , she said , and then expanded to encompass floods , tornadoes , hurricanes and other disasters . Incident command centers are now the norm during disasters . <p> Many large counties have also developed their own emergency management offices in the decades since the flood . Feyen remembers when Larimer County hired its first emergency manager -- that person worked alone and out of a cubicle in the sheriff 's office . <p> Equipment was offline . Forecasters were receiving few on-the-ground reports from the area . And the two meteorologists working that night were handling multiple incidents @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ metric in the last 50 years , state climatologist Russ Schumacher said . Radar networks are more robust , weather forecasting models are more accurate and information is more accessible to the public . <p> But accurately predicting exactly how much rain will fall and where remains a challenge , he said . It is difficult to know whether a storm will drop 4 inches or 12 inches , or whether the bulk of the rain will fall directly on a narrow canyon or a few miles south . <p> " What happened in the Big Thompson Canyon was so localized , it was so much rain in such a small area -- that 's always going to be hard to forecast , " Schumacher said . <p> Schumacher pointed to the deadly 2025 flash floods in central Texas as an example . Heavy rains flooded the Guadalupe River , which rose more than 25 feet in less than an hour . The surge killed 136 people , including more than 20 children at a summer camp . <p> It 's unclear how climate change will shape the future of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate change is expected to cause more extreme weather . Even if the average annual rainfall does n't change , the rain may fall in fewer but more intense storms . <p> " Even if we ca n't really pin down exactly how climate change is going to affect our rainfall here , we have these awful floods in history , and we know we have the chance that they will happen again , " he said . " These are the things we know can happen , and that 's why it 's important to learn the lessons from the big floods of the past . @@139334235 <p> A landmark conservation project in western Maine has officially closed , permanently protecting more than 78,000 acres in the Magalloway River region while preserving public access for hunting , fishing , paddling , snowmobiling and other outdoor recreation . <p> The project , first announced in March 2025 after conservation groups reached an agreement with Bayroot LLC and Wagner Forest Management , was contingent on raising $62 million before it could close . The completed effort connects more than 500,000 acres of contiguous conserved land across western Maine and neighboring New Hampshire through a partnership among Rangeley Lakes Heritage Trust , Forest Society of Maine , Northeast Wilderness Trust and The Nature Conservancy . <p> The conservation deal protects wildlife habitat while maintaining the region 's working forest . More than 62,500 acres will remain privately owned and managed for sustainable forestry under a permanent conservation easement held by Forest Society of Maine , preventing future development while allowing timber harvesting and forest management to continue . <p> Northeast Wilderness Trust acquired more than 11,200 acres to establish a new public wilderness preserve encompassing the remote headwaters of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Mountain . Rangeley Lakes Heritage Trust now owns and manages more than 4,400 acres , including land along the Magalloway and Little Magalloway rivers and a forest tract near Cupsuptic Lake , where it plans to restore river habitat for native brook trout . <p> The Magalloway River watershed is widely recognized as one of the nation 's last strongholds for self-sustaining native brook trout . The conservation easement also establishes additional 100-foot protective buffers along high-priority brook trout streams identified by the Maine Department of Inland Fisheries and Wildlife , while the project protects habitat for Canada lynx , moose , black bear , white-tailed deer , ruffed grouse and more than 20 species of warblers . <p> The partners also acknowledged the Magalloway lands and waters as part of the homeland of the Wabanaki Tribal Nations and said they hope the project creates new opportunities for Wabanaki people to strengthen and re-establish cultural and spiritual connections to the area . <p> An aerial view shows Parmachenee Lake in Maine 's northern forest .
